Editorial 
Opportunities from the bond sell-off  
Strategy summary: The global bond sell-off is creating tactical 
and medium-term opportunities. In the US, September’s yield 
surge has improved the risk -reward in 10 -year government 
bonds, presenting an attractive entry point for investors with a 
6-12-month horizon. We believe bond and money markets are 
overly hawkish about the Fed outlook, given disinflation is likely 
to return as impact of oil prices and tariffs ease next year. 
In equities, we stick to quality, especially sectors riding the AI -
led earnings boom. The upcoming Q3 earnings season is likely 
to show AI -investments are powering sectors beyond the 
technology and communications services, such as financials, 
materials and power and electrification. We remain constructive 
on the semiconductor and memory segments. We also expect 
the US Dollar to peak in the next few weeks along with short -
to-medium-term US bond yields. This creates opportunities for 
AUD corporate bonds, Emerging Market USD bonds and gold. 
Attractive risk -reward in US 10 -year government bonds.  
The US 10-year bond yield rose more than 50bps in September 
(biggest monthly rise since 2022) and 115bps this year, amid 
expectations of economic resilience, higher Fed rates, fuel -
driven inflation, AI-related capital demand and fiscal concerns. 
Valuations appear close to fair value, improving the 6-12-month 
risk-reward. Bond arithmetic suggests losses of less than 1% if 
the yield rises to 6%, vs. more than 10% gains if it falls to 4.5%. 
Resilient US economic activity, but softer -than-expected 
inflation. September data showed robust US employment 
(jobless claims, ADP) and core capital-goods orders. However, 
consumer confidence remains depressed, real disposable 
income is flat and households are drawing on savings to sustain 
spending. Against this divided backdrop, core PCE inflation - 
the Fed’s preferred gauge - was softer than expected at 0.2% 
m/m and 3.0% y/y. A revised method to measure PCE inflation 
also indicate that annual core PCE inflation was 35bps lower 
than previously reported, narrowing the gap with CPI inflation. 
Markets appear overly hawkish on the Fed. Money markets 
price about 85bps of tightening over the next 12 months. A 
strong labour market (September’s non -farm payrolls data is 
due tonight) may justify one or two further hikes, but aggressive 
tightening after the sharp rise in bond yields – which has 
already tightened financial conditions - could threaten financial 
stability. US small- and mid-cap equities have already fallen 8–
9% from recent peaks over the past couple of months, 
materially underperforming large caps, as energy and 
borrowing-cost concerns intensify. W hile some Fed speakers 
remain hawkish, we  agree with New York Fed President 
Williams’ that there is no urgency to tighten. We see little 
chance of an October Fed rate hike and expect inflation data 
for Sep-Nov determining whether the Fed moves in December. 
Stay with quality equities and AI beneficiaries.  US large 
caps have absorbed the rise in bond yields relatively well, 
supported by expectations of robust earnings growth led by AI-
related investment. We continue to favour quality companies 
and sectors benefiting from this AI cycle. Semiconductors and 
memory remain preferred exposures: structural AI demand 
should extend the memory upcycle, while long -term supply 
agreements provide better revenue visibility. The opportunity is 
also broadening beyond technology and communication 
services into financials, materials, power and electrification. 
Opportunities in AUD corporate bonds and gold.  The USD 
rebound alongside higher US yields offers a tactical opportunity 
to add higher -yielding Emerging Market USD and non -USD 
bonds. We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds, which 
offer a yield premium over comparable US credit. Our 
constructive medium-term AUD/USD view could add returns for 
USD-based investors over the next 6 –12 months. We would 
also use gold’s recent pullback to add exposure, supported by 
sustained Emerging Market central bank demand. We continue 
to view USD 4,000/oz as important technical support for gold.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

### Page 4

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions 
  Do you expect more RBA rate hikes after its less hawkish 
guidance? What is your view on the AUD and AUD bonds? 
Our view: We expect the RBA to leave rates unchanged, but 
retain the option to hike again should inflation intensify. We 
maintain our medium-term constructive AUD view and maintain 
our opportunistically bullish AUD corporate bonds idea. 
Rationale: The RBA’s forward guidance remains data dependent, 
explicitly allowing further tightening if needed as inflation remains too 
high.  At 4.60%, Australia’s cash rate is currently 60–85bp above the 
Fed’s 3.75–4.00% range. Thus, even with our two additional 25bp s 
Fed hikes expectation, Australian rates would hold a modest positive 
rate differential. We expect the AUD to strengthen once the Fed 
signals that US rates are near their peak, which should allow resilient 
Australian fundamentals to reassert themselves. Key risks are 
persistently high US yields, weaker China growth or a sharper 
deterioration in global risk sentiment. 
We believe risk-reward has become more attractive for AUD bonds 
following the bear -flattening of the AUD yield curve. Markets are 
already pricing in one more RBA hike through end-2027, presenting 
an attractive opportunity to earn a yield even as increasingly 
restrictive financial conditions reinforc e disinflation over time. 
Meanwhile, the positive interest rate differential with the US supports 
the currency outlook, supporting the case for AUD bonds. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  What does the incoming US data tell us about the path for 
US 10-year bond yields from here? 
Our view: The risk -reward in US 10 -year yields has turned 
attractive following their move higher amid incoming US data. 
Rationale: Recent US data has indeed started to show softer-than-
expected inflation. However, market technicals are likely temporarily 
delaying a move lower in bond yields, with reports suggesting the  
rise in yields is creating forced selling amongst hedge funds and real 
estate investment trusts. Nevertheless, we do not expect this to be 
an extended selloff. We have initiated an opportunistic bullish idea 
on US 10-year government bonds this week and continue to believe 
this idea offers an attractive risk/reward on a 6-12-month horizon as 
yields become attractive enough to entice buyer flow to return. On a 
broader allocation basis, we retain our structural preference for 3-7-
year maturities in bonds. 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds amid 
Australia-US interest rate differentials and positive 
outlook for the AUD

### Page 5

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  How should investors position in the GBP ahead of the UK 
budget announcement next month? 
Our view: We maintain a medium-term constructive GBP view, 
but keep near-term exposure measured ahead of the 28 October 
Budget. We would view any pre -Budget weakness as an 
opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure. 
Rationale: The government’s decision to adjust the pension triple 
lock from 2030 is an early indication of greater willingness to tackle 
longer-term spending pressures, reinforcing Chancellor Healey’s 
commitment to fiscal discipline and meeting the fiscal rules. Markets 
are likely to scrutinise the Budget closely for evidence that this 
discipline extends to near -term tax and spending decisions. A 
credible Budget should help contain pressure on UK government 
bond yields and support GBP. Any fiscal slippage remains a key risk.  
Near term, we prefer measured GBP exposure as elevated US yields 
and repricing towards further Fed tightening could continue to 
support the USD. Over the medium term, we expect GBP to rebound 
as the Fed eventually signals the peak of its tightening cycle. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  How would different US mid-term election outcome 
scenarios affect the outlook for US equities? 
Our view: We believe election outcomes will have a limited 
impact over the next 12 months. We remain positive on US 
equities driven by fundamental earnings growth.  
Rationale: Over the past 50 years, the ruling US President’s party 
has retained control of both chambers of Congress on only two 
occasions (1978 and 2002). All other elections result ed in an 
opposition sweep or a divided government.  
Based on the latest prediction markets, it appears that an opposition 
sweep (both the House and Senate flips from Republican to 
Democrats) is more likely than a divided government ( in which the 
House flips to Democrats , while the Senate remains under 
Republican control). A scenario where Republicans retain a majority 
is least likely per prediction markets. 
While mid -term elections may introduce near -term volatility, we 
caution against overreaction. Historically, the average return for the 
S&P 500 following an opposition sweep is 17% over the next 12 
months, but the average return following a divided government is not 
significantly worse, at 15%. We prefer to base our investment views 
on fundamental earnings growth, where investor attention should 
focus on the upcoming Q3 earnings season. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure over

### Page 6

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is the outlook for  the memory chip and broader 
semiconductors industry? 
Our view: We remain positive on semiconductors and memory 
chips. Structural AI demand should prolong the memory 
upcycle while long-term agreements improve revenue visibility. 
Rationale: Recent industry commentary points to tighter memory 
supply-demand conditions through 2028, reinforcing our view that AI 
demand is likely to extend the memory upcycle. Furthermore, adoption 
of long-term agreements improves revenue and earnings visibility. 
Despite debates around “Pacing the Frontier”, we have seen a flurry 
of new model releases, along with the introduction of new consumer 
AI agents capable of comparing prices online and making 
purchases. These are early days, but the path is open for a business-
to-agent (B2A) economy where businesses increasingly interact with 
AI assistants acting on customers’ behalf. As these applications are 
increasingly inference intensive, wider adoption should support 
demand for memory, with its share of global semiconductor 
revenues projected to rise from 30.7% in 2025 to 66.7% in 2027. 
Against this backdrop, we maintain our Opportunistic idea on Global 
Semiconductors. Given memory  chips’ cyclical nature, investors 
should avoid overconcentration in memory names and maintain a 
diversified exposure across Big Tech and semiconductors. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  Does a US bond yield stress test raise the risk to your 
constructive view on US equities? 
Our view: We have a target for the S&P 500 index of 8400 over 
the next 12 months. A scenario of a sustained rise in the US 10-
year government bond yield to 5.6% could lead to negative 
returns from the current S&P500 level (7,666). 
Rationale: Our base case 12-month target for the S&P 500 is 8,400, 
which assumes 2027 S&P 500 EPS of USD 420 and a 20x forward 
P/E multiple. A rise in bond yields would have an impact on equity 
valuations, as a higher discount rate reduces the present value of 
future earnings. W e stress -test our target under different 10 -year 
yield and earnings scenarios.  
A rise in bond yields would arguably be associated with a stronger 
growth environment and hence, even higher earnings. However, 
assuming unchanged EPS at our base case of USD 420, a 
(sustained) rise in the 10 -year bond yield to 5.6% would lead to 
negative returns from the current level  if not offset by a further 
improvement in earnings growth . However, in o ur base case  we 
expect the 10-year yield is more likely to fall from here. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist

The memory segment is projected to account for 
two-thirds of global semiconductor revenues by

Document 334: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors

Source PDF: all report pdf/Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Thematic Reports)
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 18
Status: ok

### Page 1

WS Global CIO Office

InvesTips

Roots and routes –  
A framework for Global 
Indian investors

September 2026

### Page 2

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  2

India’s financial coming of age has created a generation of investors at a 
distinctive crossroads: deeply connected to one of the world’s fastest -growing 
economies, yet increasingly aware that portfolios concentrated in a single market 
can accumulate risks over time. 
This report discusses the challenges faced by two distinct ‘Global Indian’ investor 
profiles. The first is the onshore Indian investor: a resident Indian whose wealth 
is largely denominated in domestic equities and fixed income, but who may still treat 
global markets as an occasional satellite allocation rather than a core portfolio 
component. The second is the offshore Indian investor : a non -resident Indian 
(NRI) based outside India with full access to global capital markets, yet who may 
be under-allocated to the growth story unfolding in their country of origin. Because 
global benchmarks understate India’s economic importance, investors seeking 
more meaningful participation in its growth story may consider allocating above 
benchmark levels, with NRIs’ potential stronger  affinity with India providing an 
additional reason to make this adjustment. 
For both groups, the starting point is the same: a concentrated bias towards either 
Indian or global markets can create risks that diversification can help mitigate 
without requiring investors to abandon their core convictions. For onshore Indian 
investors, global assets can provide structural currency diversification and access 
to sectors that are under -represented in Indian indices. Our Capital Market 
Assumptions (CMAs)  illustrate the potential portfolio benefits: increasing  
the allocation to global assets from 0% to 15% raises expected returns while 
reducing expected volatility meaningfully. As a result, the portfolio’s risk -adjusted 
returns improve. 
For offshore Indian investors, India offers a real yield premium, a rising weight in global indices and long-term growth that remains 
meaningfully under -represented in global benchmarks. A historical perspective illustrates the opportunity: since 2001,  
Indian equities have delivered over 10% annualised returns in USD terms, outperforming Developed Market equities over the 
same period. 
Onshore and offshore Indian investors may share the same connection to India, but they face very different portfolio challenges. 
There are key dividing lines that determine how each investor should think about diversification, risk management and the rol e 
of Indian assets within a broader investment portfolio.

Manpreet Gill 
Chief Investment Officer, EMEA 
Sylvain Huard 
Head of Asset Allocation 
Hannah Chew 
Portfolio Strategist

1 Two investor profiles, one shared 
opportunity

### Page 3

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  3

Base currency –  
the first and most 
important divide 
 The onshore Indian investor earns, spends, saves and ultimately retires in rupees (INR). Their 
relevant return is, therefore, an INR return. If the INR depreciates against the US dollar (USD), 
foreign assets rise in INR terms and can act as a natural hedge . If the INR appreciates, those 
same assets deliver lower INR returns than their local -currency performance would suggest. 
For the offshore investor, the currency problem is reversed. Their benchmark is typically USD-
based, and Indian markets are the exposure that introduces exchange-rate risk. A strong year 
for Indian equities in INR terms may look less compelling once translated back into USD at an 
unfavourable exchange rate.

Regulatory access –  
different constraints, 
different routes 
 The onshore Indian investor operates within the Liberalised Remittance Scheme (LRS) 
framework. Depending on their circumstances, this can offer meaningful or constrained access 
to global markets. Fund -of-funds and international exchange -traded funds (ETFs)  can also 
offer alternative access routes, as opposed to direct securities alone.  
For the offshore Indian investor, the challenge is less about access to India than choosing the 
most appropriate investment route. They can generally invest through onshore accounts, while 
offshore-listed funds and ETFs may offer a more convenient alternat ive. The preferred 
structure will depend on their residency, tax domicile and product eligibility, making careful 
planning important even where access itself is relatively broad. 
As an example, India’s Gujarat International Finance Tec -City (GIFT City), home to the 
country’s International Financial Services Centre (IFSC), is one potential implementation route 
available to both onshore and offshore Indian investors. For offshore investors, GIFT City offers 
an efficient way to access India-linked and global investment products with little or no need for 
INR conversion, while allowing foreign -currency repatriation. For onshore investors, it can 
facilitate USD -denominated exposure throu gh foreign -currency accounts under the LRS, 
providing a domestically regulated, yet globally oriented platform for portfolio diversification.

Portfolio risk –  
concentration on 
one side, under-
allocation on the 
other 
 The onshore Indian investor’s core risk is concentration and complacency: a portfolio almost 
entirely exposed to India’s domestic economic cycle, policy environment and sectoral 
composition. This is not a prediction that these will deteriorate, as India’s structural growth story 
remains compelling. Rather, this implies the risk is structural when all equity exposure sits 
within a single market, causing the portfolio to react simultaneously to the same domestic 
catalysts. By contrast, the offshore Indian investor faces the opposite problem: under-allocation 
to one of the world’s fastest-growing economies, driven partly by access frictions and partly by 
the difficulty of sizing a home-market allocation through a global investment framework.

Fig. 1

can be reduced or bolstered when translated back 
to USD

structures or the Fully Accessible Route (FAR)

diversify sector, cycle and currency exposure

deliberate growth and real-yield overweight

dependence and limited sector breadth

operational complexity

Source: Standard Chartered

### Page 4

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  4

A diversified portfolio makes sense 
regardless of the investor profile 
Before examining the specific assets available to Global 
Indian investors, it is useful to step back and ask a more 
fundamental question: why hold multiple asset classes at all? 
The simple answer is diversification. Because different asset 
classes play dif ferent roles and perform differently across 
economic and market conditions, combining them thoughtfully 
can reduce risk, create more consistent outcomes and build 
greater portfolio resilience than relying on any single asset 
class alone. 
Each major asset class tends to perform best under different 
economic conditions. Equities usually benefit when corporate 
earnings are growing and investor confidence is strong, but 
they can fall sharply when growth disappoints or credit 
conditions tighten. Bonds can provide income and may hold 
their value, or even appreciate, when growth slows and 
central banks ease policy, but they typically face pressure 
when inflation is high and interest rates rise. Gold has long 
been viewed as a geopolitical and infla tion hedge, and its 
relatively low correlation with equities and bonds can make it 
a useful portfolio diversifier. 
In practice, a portfolio concentrated in a single asset class – 
whether Indian equities, USD cash or any other standalone 
exposure – is implicitly relying on one set of economic and 
market conditions remaining favourable. A thoughtfully 
constructed multi-asset portfolio does not need to be the best 
performer in every year, but it is also unlikely to be the worst. 
Its purpose is to build resilience across market cycles: 
capturing growth when conditions are supportive, limiting 
downside when stress emerges, and compounding steadily 
over time in a way that no single asset class can consistently 
achieve on its own.

A practical asset-class toolkit for Global 
Indian investors 
Developed Market (DM) equities 
These are typically shares in large, established companies 
across the US, Europe, the UK and Japan and usually 
represent the largest and most liquid equity markets in the 
world. They are home to the technology giants, global 
healthcare leaders and consumer  brands that dominate their 
sectors globally – many of which have no equivalent in Indian 
indices. DM equities provide the broadest possible access to 
global corporate earnings growth, and for onshore investors, 
they provide USD and other major currency ex posure that 
structurally helps hedge against INR depreciation. For 
offshore investors, DM equities are the natural portfolio core, 
given their base currency and broad exposure of the asset 
class in global markets. 
Emerging Markets (EM) equities  
EM equities span a broad universe of developing economies, 
including China, South Korea, Taiwan, Brazil and other major 
EM markets. They offer greater growth potential than DM 
equities, but also tend to be more volatile. Their returns are 
supported by risi ng consumption, commodity cycles and the 
expansion of industry and the middle class. For Indian 
investors, EM equities provide exposure to regional peers that 
behave differently from Indian domestic markets, adding a 
layer of diversification within the equity sleeve.

1 The building blocks of a Global 
Indian portfolio

### Page 5

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  5

Indian equities  
Indian listed equities can be split up into two distinct sub-asset 
classes: 1) small - and mid -cap equities and 2) large -cap 
equities. The latter represent India’s established blue -chip 
companies with strong balance sheets and have relatively 
lower volatility within the Indian equity spectrum. These are 
typically the anchor of any Indian equity allocation. 
Meanwhile, small - and mid -cap equities tap into a different 
part of India’s growth story: the domestic consumption engine, 
the formalisation of the inform al economy and the 
entrepreneurial mid -market that scales rapidly in a high -
growth environment. They offer meaningfully higher potential 
returns, but also comes with higher volatility and lower 
liquidity, making the appropriate allocation dependent on the 
investor’s risk tolerance and investment time horizon.  
DM Investment Grade bonds 
Developed market investment grade bonds encompass two 
closely related asset classes that play a core defensive role in 
portfolios. Government bonds, such as US Treasuries, 
German Bunds, and UK Gilts, are issued by highly rated 
sovereigns and are widely regarded as the closest equivalent 
to a risk -free asset within global markets. Investment grade 
corporate bonds, issued by financially strong companies rated 
BBB- or above, offer higher yields than government bonds in 
return for accepting a modest amount of credit risk. 
Together, these assets provide an important source of portfolio 
stability. Government bonds have historically performed well 
during periods of economic uncertainty and market turbulence, 
helping to cushion equity losses when investors seek safer 
assets. In vestment grade corporate bonds enhance overall 
income generation while maintaining relatively low volatility, 
allowing investors to improve return potential without taking on 
the higher risks typically associated with equities. 
EM USD bonds 
EM USD bonds are USD -denominated debt issued by EM 
sovereign and quasi-sovereign borrowers, including countries 
such as Indonesia, Mexico, Brazil and South Africa. Their 
USD denomination removes direct exposure to the issuers’ 
local currencies, making them  a more accessible entry point 
into EM fixed income than local -currency bonds, particularly 
for offshore investors with hard -currency liabilities. For 
onshore investors, EM USD bonds add hard -currency fixed-
income exposure and can diversify portfolios conc entrated in 
Indian equities and INR interest -rate risk. However, their 
returns still depend on global interest rates, credit spreads and 
investor risk appetite. 
India government bonds  
The Indian bond market offers something that is increasingly 
rare globally: a genuine positive real (net -of-inflation) yield. 
India’s 10-year government bond yielded around 6.7% in mid-
2026 against a headline inflation of about 4.4%.  This 
represents a positive real yield of nearly 2.8% that compares 
favourably to most DM alternatives. India’s inclusion in global 
bond indices is now also channelling systematic passive 
inflows into this market, providing an additional structural 
demand tailwind.  
Gold 
Gold occupies a unique role in this framework particularly for 
Indian investors. Beyond its universal function as an inflation 
and geopolitical hedge, gold in INR terms has historically 
delivered strong returns during periods of INR weakness 
because gold prices are globally denominated in USD. When 
the INR depreciates, the INR value of gold rises, providing a 
natural cushion against the same currency dynamics that 
erode domestic purchasing power.

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  6

The case for global exposure for onshore Indian investors  – Most resident 
Indian investors already diversify across asset classes, typically holding a mix of 
equities, fixed income and, in some cases, commodities such as gold. However, 
this diversification is usually implemented through domestic assets. The gap, 
therefore, is not necessarily a lack of asset-class breadth, but a lack of geographic 
diversification. This is where global investing can play an important role: by adding 
exposure to different economies, currencies, sectors and market cycles  
beyond India.  
Argument

Since India’s market liberalisation in the early 1990s, the INR has depreciated significantly 
against the USD, weakening from approximately INR 31 per USD in 1994 to more than INR 95 
per USD by August 2026 – an annualised depreciation rate of 3.4%. This long-term depreciation 
is not simply a market fluctuation; it reflects a structural feature of an economy that has 
persistently run higher inflation than the US. For investors holding only INR-denominated assets, 
this creates a gradual erosion of global purchasing power over time. USD-denominated foreign 
assets can, therefore, serve as a structural currency hedge, helping preserve international 
purchasing power rather than representing a speculative currency bet. 
The INR 
depreciates 
structurally

Argument

Indian equity indices remain heavily weighted towards the financial, consumer discretionary and 
industrial sectors. By contrast, technology – the largest sector in global DM indices – has a much 
smaller representation in India’s listed market, despite the scale and importance of India’s 
broader technology economy. This gap has become more significant with the rise of artificial 
intelligence (AI). While India has strong technology talent and growing AI adoption, Indian 
markets offer little -to-no exposure to technology in the investible equity market. As a result, 
investors with portfolios concentrated in Indian equities have limited exposure to global 
technology leaders. Adding DM equities, therefore, provides sector diversification, including 
greater partici pation in the growth opportunities associated with AI, as well as geographic 
diversification.

Argument

Indian equities respond sharply to domestic catalysts: RBI rate decisions, monsoon outcomes, 
fiscal borrowing announcements and India -specific political developments. When these factors 
turn negative, they tend to affect domestic equity positions simultane ously. Global equity 
markets are not driven by the same catalysts, so a domestic Indian growth slowdown does not 
cause US corporate earnings to fall or other global equities to retreat. Adding a global equity 
sleeve means that not all equity exposure moves in tandem during a domestic downturn.  
The same logic applies to bonds. Indian bonds respond primarily to RBI monetary policy, 
domestic inflation and India’s fiscal borrowing calendar. These drivers are largely independent 
of the global rate cycle. An investor holding only Indian equities and Indian bonds has all of their 
assets exposed to the same set of domestic macro factors at once. A portfolio that blends Indian 
and global assets reduces this localised cycle dependency, spreading exposure across markets 
that follow different cycles. 
Market cycles are 
not synchronised

### Page 9

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  9

The case for Indian asset exposure for offshore Indian investors  – India 
stands out as one of the few major economies where 6 -7% annual real GDP 
growth remains a credible base case through the end of the decade. For offshore 
investors, the allocation case is not built on growth alone, but on four reinforcing 
pillars: a structural growth premium unmatched among large economies; reforms 
that have improved the quality and breadth of that growth; a demographic 
dividend that still has years to run; and a digital public infrastructure revolution 
that is lifting productivity at population scale.

The growth premium  
According to the International Monetary Fund, India remains the world’s fastest-growing major economy. Real 
GDP expanded by 7.6% in 2025 and is projected to grow by 6.5% in 2026 – outpacing China at 4.4%, G7 
economies at 1.6%, and EM and developing economies at 3.9%. This is not a one-year outlier. Apart from the 
Covid-19 pandemic shock, India has sustained a 6 -8% real GDP growth range for much of the past decade, 
reinforcing its structural growth premium. 
This structural growth is also reflected in equity -market performance. Since 2001, Indian equities have 
delivered annualised returns of 10 .1% in USD terms , outperforming DM equities, which returned 7.5% 
annualised over the same period. This long-term track record reinforces the case for including Indian equities 
as a deliberate source of growth within an offshore investor’s portfolio. 
For offshore investors, there is a clear benchmark gap. A global equity benchmark allocation of around 1.3% 
to India materially understates the country’s economic weight. India accounts for roughly 8.2% of global GDP 
on a purchasing power parity basis in 2025, which is significantly higher than its equity index weight. As Indian 
capital markets deepen and foreign ownership constraints ease, this gap between economic weight and index 
representation should continue to narrow, while rising benchmark inclusion can create a recurring source of 
passive inflows into Indian assets.

Economic and market reforms 
India's growth story over the past decade has not just been about the pace of GDP expansion. It also reflects 
a series of market -oriented reforms that have strengthened the investment environment and made growth 
more durable and accessible to long -term cap ital. Examples include the Goods and Services Tax, the 
Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, the Real Estate Regulation and Development Act and measures to deepen 
and formalise domestic capital markets. Taken together, these reforms have improved the structural 
competitiveness of the Indian economy in ways that show up in productivity growth, foreign direct investment 
inflows and market depth – not just in headline GDP numbers.

1 Why Indian assets still belong in every 
offshore Indian investor portfolio

### Page 10

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  10

Demographic dividend  
India’s demographic advantage is a long -term structural tailwind, not a short -term population bulge. The 
country has the world’s largest working -age population and, with a median age of around 29.2 (according to 
Worldometer’s latest data), remains one of the youngest major economies. As more Indians enter the formal 
workforce, earn higher incomes and build financial savings, they can support a broader domestic consumption 
base, deeper financial intermediation and continued cap ital-market growth. The shift in household savings 
from physical assets, such as gold and real estate, towards financial assets reinforces this trend. Monthly 
systematic investment plan (SIP) inflows into mutual funds, which have risen from approximately INR 36.6bn 
in 2016 to more than IN R 310bn in 2026, are a clear expression of this demographic and savings transition. 
They also create a durable, domestically -sourced demand base for Indian equities that is less dependent on 
foreign capital flows. 
However, the demographic dividend is not automatic; its realisation will depend on equipping a growing 
workforce with employable, industry -relevant skills and creating sufficient productive jobs. India has made 
meaningful investments in this direction thro ugh the Skill India Mission, including vocational training, 
apprenticeships and courses aligned with emerging sectors. Sustained improvements in training quality, 
employer alignment and job creation will, therefore, be critical to convert India’s demographic scale into lasting 
gains in productivity, income and economic growth.

Digital infrastructure 
India's digital public infrastructure – collectively known as the India Stack – has built a low-cost, inter-operable 
rails system that is accelerating formalisation, financial inclusion and commercial productivity at a speed with 
few global parallels. Exam ples include Aadhaar for identity, the Unified Payments Interface for payments, 
DigiLocker for documentation and the Open Network for Digital Commerce. Together, these platforms are 
productivity infrastructure: reducing the cost of doing business, formalising the informal economy and 
expanding the addressable market for financial services companies, fintechs, consumer platforms and logistics 
businesses in ways that compound into earnings growth over time.

The India fixed income opportunity 
India's 10-year government bond yielded around 6.7% in mid -2026, against headline consumer inflation of 
approximately 4.4% – implying a real yield of around 2.4%. This compares to near-zero or negative real yields 
in much of the developed world over the past decade and to real yields of 1-2% available in most comparable 
EM bond markets.

For onshore Indian investors, adding global assets can improve portfolio 
resilience without diluting expected INR returns. Our modelling shows that moving 
from a fully domestic portfolio to a 15% global allocation lifts the expected return 
only marginally,  from 8.42% to 8.44%, but reduces expected volatility more 
meaningfully, from 18.38% to 16.86%. This improves the risk-adjusted return from 
0.46 to 0.50. The benefit comes from exposure to markets, sectors and currencies 
that do not move in lockstep with I ndian assets, helping reduce domestic 
concentration risk while adding access to different structural growth drivers.

### Page 12

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  12

For offshore investors with globally diversified portfolios, Indian assets are best viewed as a deliberate 
strategic tilt towards a differentiated source of long -term growth, rather than as a near -term portfolio 
efficiency trade. Based on our forward -looking Capital Market Assumptions (CMAs), increasing the 
allocation to Indian assets to 15% leaves the expected USD return broadly unchanged at 6.10%, while 
expected volatility rises from 10.32% to 11.00%, reflecting the additional EM and currency risks.  
However, these aggregate estimates may not fully capture the opportunity in Indian financial market assets. 
Our CMAs show that the case for Indian equities is compelling when viewed from a returns perspective 
alone. 
The challenge to portfolio efficiency arises from Indian equities’ relatively high expected volatility. For 
context, our CMAs suggest an expected volatility of 29% for Indian large -cap equities. This number is 
considerably higher than the level experienced over the last 10 years (18.9%) and modestly higher than the 
level over the past several decades. This is a key point because the drag on risk-adjusted returns in Fig. 8 
comes from the higher expected volatility of Indian equities rather than any challenge to the expected return. 
As an example, Fig. 9 illustrates that calibrating Indian equity volatility to more recent market experience 
produces a more balanced assessment of portfolio risk and return. Using recent history as an example 
shows that risk-adjusted returns actually improve modestly from 0.79 to 0.81.  
Overall, this means the debate is less about how high a return Indian equities are expected to deliver over 
the coming years. Instead, how volatile Indian assets are likely to be is key to whether adding them to global 
portfolios improves risk-adjusted returns.

Pulling this together, we would argue that a strong case exists for a measured allocation to Indian assets –
particularly equities – exists, in our view, provided two conditions are met.
First, investors should retain conviction that the structural drivers of India’s return premium – including
earnings growth, improving governance and an expanding investable universe – remain intact. This is key

to being comfortable with the expected return of Indian equities and bonds.
Second, investors must be willing and able to tolerate the volatility associated with Indian equities. While
one could argue that the assumed volatility in our CMAs is high relative to the level experienced in recent
decades, under most scenarios, it would remain meaningfully higher than that of DM equities.
Taken together, offshore Indian investors should consider incorporating Indian assets within a globally

diversified portfolio. However, the allocation should be deliberate and sized in line with their objectives and
risk tolerance, reflecting a conscious investment view and not just the familiarity of a home bias.

These results show that cross-border diversification serves different needs for different investors but leads
to the same portfolio conclusion. For onshore Indian investors, global assets can help lower volatility,

broaden sector exposure and provide a structural hedge against INR depreciation. For offshore Indian
investors, India offers a deliberate growth and real -yield allocation that is under -represented in global
benchmarks. In both cases, combining Indian and global assets can build a more resil ient portfolio across
market cycles.

### Page 14

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  14

Resident onshore Indian investors and offshore Indian investors begin from opposite starting points. One 
is anchored in India and looking outward; the other is anchored globally and looking inward. Their regulatory 
regimes, base currencies and available instruments differ. Yet the portfolio destination is the same: 
meaningful exposure to both India and the world. 
That conclusion reflects the reality of an interconnected investment world. Indian corporate earnings are 
shaped by global commodity prices, technology supply chains and demand from DMs. At the same time, 
companies in the US, Europe and Japan are exposed to the consumption growth of emerging economies, 
including India. Bond markets may respond first to domestic central banks, but they are still influenced by 
global rate cycles; gold is priced in USD, yet its impact is felt across currencies. No investor can  afford to 
treat the other half of the world as irrelevant. 
What differs is not the principle, but the path. The onshore Indian investor adds global exposure as a 
deliberate extension of an India -centric portfolio, seeking additional return opportunities while gaining a 
structural hedge against INR depreciation. Th e offshore investor builds India exposure as a deliberate 
overweight above global benchmarks, accepting INR volatility as the cost of accessing India’s growth 
premium and real-yield opportunity. The appropriate allocation will differ by profile and risk appetite, but the 
core message is the same: both investors need both markets.

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

The end of the safety net:  
Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

28 September 2026 
For several decades, financial markets and the wider economy benefited from an implicit 
safety net. If a slowdown or a downturn arrived, there was a monetary put from central 
banks, and a fiscal put from governments. The monetary put would see interest rates 
slashed and liquidity injected via QE. The fiscal put would see governments run larger 
deficits to cushion any downturn. These policy stabilisers smoothed out the business 
cycle, and supported asset valuations too. 
These policy interventions were increasingly normalised from the late-1980s, and became 
more aggressive with each passing cycle. This reached its peak during the Covid-19 
pandemic, when governments ran fiscal deficits unprecedented in peacetime, whilst 
many central banks expanded their balance sheets with huge asset purchases. 
But after an era of ever-bigger interventions, these old stabilisers now face major 
obstacles. On the fiscal side, policy is constrained today by sovereign yields at multi-year 
highs, alongside the highest debt-to-GDP ratios in several decades. Then on the monetary 
side, policy is constrained by persistent inflation that’s increasingly supply-driven. 
This represents a big shift from the last few decades, but it does echo previous regimes 
where the macroeconomic stabilisers were either blunted or left unused. A good example 
is the 1970s experience, when stagflation and supply shocks posed a dilemma to 
traditional policy tools. Even further back, the pre-WWI environment of the classical gold 
standard and balanced budget orthodoxy saw governments play a very different and more 
limited role in macroeconomic stabilisation. 
Looking forward, this environment means policymakers will have a much more difficult 
time stabilising the business cycle in the coming years. So absent a sharp fall in yields or a 
collapse in inflation, higher macro volatility will be here to stay.

### Page 2

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
1. Background: How the stabilisers used to work.
Economic policymakers have long used interventions to stabilise output. This
became increasingly fashionable after WWII, when Keynesian economics
established a more active use for fiscal policy to stimulate the economy in a
downturn. This meant budget deficits became a more regular feature of life, rather
than used solely for wartime emergencies.

It was from the late 1980s, however, that the extent of policy interventions became
increasingly sizeable. Indeed, it was here that the origins of a central bank “put” for
financial markets began, in the crisis of Black Monday in 1987. That was a huge
stock market crash, where the S&P 500 fell over 20% in a single day, marking the
biggest daily decline in its entire history. The size of the falls even went beyond the
Great Depression, raising fears of wider contagion, particularly if it were to spark a

liquidity crisis that caused financial institutions to fail. However, the Federal
Reserve responded by cutting rates and providing liquidity, offering a
macroeconomic stabiliser against the prospect of wider economic stress. This
offered support against a wider downturn, and the US economic expansion
continued until 1990.
Whilst the genesis of a central bank “put” came in 1987, the idea gained further
traction in the late-1990s, with the Asian financial crisis in 1997, followed by the

Russian financial crisis in 1998. Fears of an economic contraction mounted,
particularly after issues at Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) raised concerns
that the US wasn’t immune from these global pressures. So once again, the Fed
delivered three 25bp rate cuts pre-emptively, seeking to support the economy.
A few years later, the bursting of the dot com bubble in early 2000 set the stage for
yet further stimulus. As equity markets declined, the Fed began to cut rates from

early 2001, ultimately delivering 475bps of cuts that year that took rates from 6.5%
to just 1.75%. That was followed up by further rate cuts in 2002 and 2003, leaving
rates at 1% by mid-2003 into 2004. Moreover, the Bush tax cuts were also delivered
in 2001 and 2003, acting as a fiscal stimulus as the wider US economy entered
recession.
In hindsight, these moves seem modest compared to what then happened in 2008,
when the Global Financial Crisis led to even bigger policy interventions. Suddenly,

the world faced the biggest economic contraction since the Great Depression, with
genuine fears that a repeat might happen. To avoid this, policymakers launched into
the biggest economic stimulus ever seen in a peacetime environment. That
included massive fiscal stimulus, with the US federal deficit at 9.8% of GDP in 2008,
the highest since 1945. Moreover, central banks slashed rates to the zero lower
bound, and many like the Federal Reserve launched into Quantitative Easing

programmes, purchasing assets to ease financial conditions even more. Over the
coming years, many began pledging to keep rates low well into the future,
pioneering the concept of forward guidance.
These levels of policy intervention weren’t confined to the immediate crisis
however. In the subsequent years, active attempts were made to prolong and
extend the business cycle, particularly as the 2010s became an era of low inflation
and low interest rates. In this environment, borrowing was historically cheap, and

low inflation meant central banks could cut rates without seeing a big overshoot of
their targets. Indeed, both the Fed and the ECB undershot their 2% inflation target
on average in the 2010s, and the ECB took their own deposit rate into negative
territory in 2014.

By 2019, the year before the pandemic, the intellectual climate had continued to
move in the direction of more policy intervention. Government bond yields hit new

lows, with Germany’s 10-year bund yield at -0.7% in summer 2019. This
environment of negative yields meant borrowing was basically a “free lunch”, and
that summer also saw the Fed begin a 75bp easing cycle, as inflation was below
their target and they wanted to sustain the expansion.
The peak of this policy interventionism was seen with the Covid-19 pandemic in
2020. Just as in 2008, the world was facing a huge and sudden economic
contraction. Moreover, the consensus among policymakers was that they could

have done more to speed up the recovery after 2008, potentially avoiding years of
very high unemployment. As such, 2020 saw the most aggressive economic
stimulus packages ever seen in a peacetime environment, well above those in 2008.
Central banks immediately slashed rates back to the zero lower bound, pledged to
keep rates low for years to come, and launched into major QE programmes again.
Then on the fiscal side, record peacetime deficits were run. Indeed, the US federal

deficit of 14.5% of GDP in 2020 was the highest since 1945.

### Page 4

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
During the pandemic period, many initially expected these interventions to remain
for years to come. After all, in mid-2020, financial markets continued to price a
continuation of the low inflation and low interest rates of the 2010s. This belief was
clear on several fronts, and the 10-year Treasury yield closed at just 0.51% in August
2020. Meanwhile, the Federal Reserve even adjusted its own framework to allow for

average inflation targeting, i.e. permitting an overshoot of the inflation target after
periods of an undershoot. This reflected the consensus view that inflation was
unlikely to be a serious threat.
But as we now know, inflation returned in a serious way after the pandemic, forcing
central banks into rate hikes. Meanwhile, public debt-to-GDP ratios rose
significantly thanks to the pandemic stimulus, helping to push up sovereign bond
yields too. In other words, many of the usual policy tools of the previous few

decades were either blunted or no longer available.
2. The Monetary Constraint: High inflation means the central
bank put isn’t available
In the pre-2020 world described above, central banks regularly grappled with how
to generate enough inflation. In part, this was thanks to several structural forces
that put downward pressure on global prices. For instance, deepening
globalisation acted as a positive supply shock that kept costs down. Meanwhile, the

expansion of the global labour force in these years also boosted the supply side. In
other words, inflation was low and central banks generally had the space to cut
interest rates when they needed to.
But since the pandemic, we have entered a very different environment of above-
target inflation across many of the world’s biggest economies. Initially, that was
caused by a huge wave of pent-up demand after the pandemic, alongside
disruption to supply chains and labour markets. But the subsequent years also

brought further shocks. Those included Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in early 2022,
driving a big spike in European energy prices, and pushing Euro Area inflation above
10% briefly. In 2026, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz then acted as a fresh energy
shock, pushing inflation further away from target and contributing to a fresh
tightening cycle from the major central banks.
Figure 4: In the 2020s, inflation has generally been above central bank targets

(PCE inflation for the US, HICP inflation for the Euro Area)

### Page 5

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Alongside those shocks, the structural forces keeping inflation down have gone
into reverse. For instance, demographic trends have seen working-age populations
shrink in many places. Meanwhile, the trend towards deeper globalisation has
stalled, and the focus has turned to near-shoring and supply-chain resilience, rather
than locating production in the cheapest place. Simultaneously, other trends like

the push for higher defence spending are also adding to demand-side pressures.
Because of all this, the 2020s so far have brought persistently above-target inflation,
and central bankers have found it much more difficult to ease policy in response to
growth wobbles or financial stability fears.
This initially became apparent in 2022, amidst the biggest inflation wave for several
decades. Central banks had to keep hiking rates, even in the face of slowing growth

and financial market stress. Indeed, despite the S&P 500 being in bear market
territory by mid-2022, the Federal Reserve still launched into a series of 75bp hikes,
the biggest hikes since the 1990s. Then in 2023, despite the collapse of Silicon
Valley Bank and fears of wider financial contagion, the Fed and ECB still continued
with their rate hikes to combat inflation.
That tightening cycle in 2022-23 was a significant moment, because high inflation

meant monetary policy could not simply act as a stabiliser against growth or
financial stress. After all, policymakers realised that if they didn’t act to tackle high
inflation, they risked a repeat of the 1970s and the potential for even harsher
monetary tightening to get inflation under control again.
Today in 2026, this is a familiar problem again. We are in a globally synchronised
hiking cycle, with the Fed, ECB and Bank of Japan all having delivered rate hikes.

So far at least, global growth has been robust and financial conditions remain
accommodative by historical standards. But because inflation remains clearly
above target, central banks don’t have the space they did in the pre-pandemic world
to simply cut rates at the first sign of difficulty. There is still an inflation mandate to
be achieved, and given we have now seen multiple years of elevated inflation, there
is a much higher risk of inflation expectations becoming unanchored in that

context, particularly if another shock occurred.
3. The Fiscal Constraint: High yields and high public debt
mean fiscal stimulus isn’t easily available either
Just as monetary policy is constrained by high inflation, fiscal policy today is also
constrained by higher yields and higher public debt. This is a big transition from the
world of 1980-2020, when yields were generally falling throughout. Indeed, despite
all the fiscal stimulus around the GFC, the continuous decline in yields over the

2010s meant that debt dynamics remained manageable, because lower yields
helped to offset the impact of higher debt.
Since the pandemic however, we’ve been in a very different world, as the dual
forces of higher debt and higher yields have made debt dynamics far more
unfavourable around the world. And this isn’t just a nominal story, as real yields
have also risen to multi-year highs as well.

### Page 6

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG

In essence, there are 4 new constraints around fiscal stimulus.
First, is the significant rise in global yields. By definition, the cost of incurring debt
has grown, in both nominal and real terms. Relative to the pre-pandemic baseline

in early 2020, the 10-year US Treasury yield has risen from 1.9% to 5.2%. Similarly
in Germany, the 10-year bund yield has risen from -0.2% to 3.6%. Moreover, with
yields already elevated, any further borrowing risks crowding out the private sector,
and further dampening growth. And for governments, interest spending has
ramped up significantly. For instance, US net interest spending as a share of GDP
exceeded its recent peak from the early 1990s in the most recent fiscal year, and the

CBO forecast this is set to continue.
Figure 6: US Net Interest Costs (% of GDP) with CBO Forecasts
0
1

3
4
5

1970 1980 1990 2000 2010 2020 2030 2040 2050
Source : CBO, Deutsche Bank
The second constraint is that public debt itself is now at a higher level. This interacts
with the higher yields mentioned above, because even though yields have only
returned to their levels from the mid-2000s, that is much more impactful because
they are being paid on a much larger debt pile. Using IMF numbers for consistency,

net public debt across the advanced economies has risen from 52% of GDP in 2000,
to 80% in 2025, with further increases projected to 2030. This means the fiscal
space available to governments is more limited than it used to be.

### Page 7

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
The third constraint on fiscal stimulus is the high starting level of budget deficits. So
to generate a meaningful fiscal stimulus, it would require deficits to be pushed up
to even higher levels than they are today. But that in turn would add to the risks of
bond market disruption that cascades through the economy, leading to higher
borrowing costs for corporates and consumers as well.
The fourth constraint is higher inflation. When inflation is high, any fiscal stimulus

that boosts demand risks pushing up prices rather than boosting output, and in turn
makes monetary tightening more likely that could offset the impact of the stimulus.
This trade-off was apparent in 2022, when central banks were hiking rates
aggressively, and even further back in the inflationary environment of the 1970s. In
a world of high inflation and supply constraints, fiscal stimulus is more likely to
overheat the economy.
4. Are there any other eras of history where the stabilisers

weren’t available?
Whilst history never quite repeats exactly, there have been other eras in which the
traditional policy stabilisers were either unavailable or severely blunted, which hold
some lessons for our own time.
The pre-WWI classical gold standard
Before WWI, the international monetary system was known as the gold standard,
in which currencies were convertible into a fixed quantity of gold. In practice
however, this acted as a huge constraint on macroeconomic stabilisation. After all,

monetary policy had to maintain the convertibility of gold at a fixed parity. This
meant contemporary options like QE weren’t available in a crisis. Meanwhile on the
fiscal side, governments generally adhered to balanced budget orthodoxy. This was
before Keynes wrote the General Theory, so the modern understanding of
countercyclical deficit spending was not in place. In the US, there wasn’t even a
central bank until the Federal Reserve’s establishment in 1913.

Figure 7: Gold Prices (US Dollars per ounce, log scale)

### Page 8

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Without modern policy stabilisers however, this was also an era of huge
macroeconomic volatility. There was a major financial panic across Europe and the
US in 1873, which marked the start of the so-called “Long Depression”. In the US,
that contraction lasted until 1879, which in length was even longer than the Great
Depression contraction of 1929-33. And this was followed by other major panics,

including the 1893 panic that led to another US contraction, along with the 1907
panic that saw the Dow Jones almost halve relative to its 1906 peak.
The stagflationary 1970s
The more recent example comes from the 1970s, another era of multiple supply
shocks. The biggest were the two oil shocks in 1973 and 1979, which both
represented negative supply shocks that pushed up inflation and hurt growth in the
US and Europe. However, this meant policymakers faced a dilemma. If central

banks cut rates, that would add to the inflationary pressures and risk worsening the
wage-price spirals already underway. Meanwhile, if fiscal stimulus were pursued,
that would also push up inflation.
Figure 8: US CPI (year-on-year % change)

1955 1960 1965 1970 1975 1980 1985
First Oil Shock
Second Oil Shock
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
This context meant that policy couldn’t smooth out the business cycle as
successfully as it had in other decades. Indeed, in the decade from the first oil shock

in 1973, the US experienced 3 separate recessions. That is significantly more
turbulent than the last 40 years, when there have been just 4 recessions in total, or
once a decade on average. For financial markets too, the 1970s were a terrible
period for asset performance, with both equities and bonds losing ground in real
terms across multiple countries.
5. Implications
If economic policymakers can’t use traditional monetary and fiscal policy tools to

stabilise output and smooth the business cycle, then this will result in higher macro
volatility.
If we look back at the last 40 years, it is notable that the 5 most recent US expansions
all rank in the top 7 longest expansions since business-cycle dating began. This has
several causes, and some of it reflects a move away from an agriculture-based
economy where output was dependent on factors like the weather. But a key role
has also been played by pre-emptive policy interventions, such as cutting rates to

stop a slowdown turning into a recession. Indeed, recent examples include the a
dovish shifts in monetary policy after the 1987 stock market crash, the 1997-98

### Page 9

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
financial crises, and the mid-cycle slowdown of 2015-16.
Beyond the actual policy interventions, these moves also had a confidence effect
that supported financial markets. The fact that policymakers stood ready to take
action helped create a virtuous circle, as optimism on the economic outlook
boosted financial asset values, supported wealth effects, and avoided a tightening
in financial conditions that would itself cause growth to slow.

Clearly, policy is not as constrained today as in some periods of history. There is no

longer a gold standard where the convertibility needs to be maintained. And unlike
the 1970s, inflation is not in double-digits. Nevertheless, this is still a more
constrained world than we have been used to in the last 4 decades. Higher inflation
and more frequent supply shocks are constraining monetary policy. Meanwhile,
higher yields and higher public debt levels are constraining fiscal policy. We have
already seen the impacts since the pandemic, most notably in 2022, when central

banks hiked rates to combat the inflation surge, and there was a simultaneous
selloff across bonds and equities of a kind unseen in modern times.
Looking forward, this environment will offer new challenges of the sort we haven't
been used to in recent decades. The policy trade-offs are now more acute, and if
pre-emptive stabilisation is no longer available, then higher macro volatility lies
ahead.

摩根⼠丹利分析师 Erik W. Woodring 团队渠道核查显⽰，东芝的扩产规模被市场严重⾼估 —— 菲

与此同时，该⾏渠道还挖出两项额外的看多信号： HDD 需求在近⼀⾄两个⽉明显提速，新兴客

⼾群体（包括新兴云⼚商和实体 AI 公司）持续涌现；未签⻓约的现货近线存储交易价格已达每
GB 3 ⾄ 5 美分，部分成交价甚⾄更⾼，远超当前合约均价 1.5 美分，为该⾏⽜市盈利预测提供了
现实佐证。
扩产规模被⾼估，时间线存疑

据该⾏了解，此次产能翻倍⽬标仅针对东芝菲律宾拉古纳科技园（ Laguna Technopark ）⼀处
⼯⼚，该⼯⼚此前主要⽣产⾮近线硬盘，仅占东芝整体产能的三分之⼆。若以 2025 财年为基
准，两年内翻倍折算为年化增速约 30% ，与⾏业整体供给增速持平，与加速增⻓的需求相⽐仍
有明显缺⼝。
摩根⼠丹利估计，即便东芝如期完成⽬标，两年内新增近线存储容量也不⾜ 150EB ，与单独希
捷⼀家通过 HAMR 技术实现约 25% 年均近线 EB 增⻓相⽐，规模⼗分有限。
值得注意的是，此次扩产计划早已在业内知晓，并⾮新动向，仅是在东芝菲律宾⼦公司成⽴ 30
周年纪念活动上被公开提及，⽇经随后对报道中的时间节点描述进⾏了更正。

渠道⼈⼠直⾔： " 这条消息不会改变任何事情。 " 此外，东芝所需的磁头和磁碟等核⼼部件依赖
TDK 和 Resonac 外部供应，多名联系⼈认为供应链协同将是制约实际产能落地的主要瓶颈。
30% 市占率⽬标是旧账，实际空间有限
⽇经报道中援引的东芝 " 近期实现 30% 市占率 " ⽬标，同样引发市场担忧。摩根⼠丹利指出，该⽬
标早在 2022 年便已提出，并⾮新近制定，⽇经不过是将这段旧表述纳⼊报道之中。
从数学逻辑看，东芝年化约 30% 的产能增速与⾏业整体供给增速相当，根本不具备⼤幅抢占份
额的条件。

定价与竞争格局未变，供应纪律依然成⽴
摩根⼠丹利渠道核查的最关键结论是：希捷和西部数据均未调整各⾃的产能计划，东芝亦未采

划，且部分资本⽀出已提前落账。渠道⼈⼠指出，东芝的合理市占率⽬标上限约在 " 低⾄中双位
数 " 区间，不构成对⾏业定价的实质冲击。
需求加速与现货价格飙升提供额外上⾏空间
在澄清东芝相关疑虑之外，摩根⼠丹利的渠道调研还发现两项对 HDD 板块构成额外⽀撑的信
号。
其⼀， HDD 需求在近⼀⾄两个⽉明显提速。
摩根⼠丹利认为，智能体 AI （ agentic AI ）是新兴的增量需求驱动⼒ —— 相较于传统聊天机器⼈
⼯作负载， AI 智能体需在多次交互间持续保留更多数据和上下⽂信息，显著增加 HDD 存储密度
需求。与此同时，需求来源也在扩⼤：由于 CSP 存储容量趋紧且 NAND 闪存价格仍为 HDD 的 17
⾄ 20 倍，新兴云⼚商和⼈形机器⼈ / 实体 AI 公司已成为 HDD 的新买家，但受制于供给缺⼝（今年
约 300EB ， 2027/2028 年预计扩⼤⾄ 400EB ），这⼀群体的采购难度较⾼。

其⼆，现货定价持续超预期。
摩根⼠丹利此前于今年 6 ⽉调研时，记录到 HDD ⼚商将近线存储定价⽬标设定为 2027/2028 年达
到每 GB 2.5 ⾄ 3 美分（当前约 1.5 美分）。最新渠道信息显⽰，未签约的近线现货交易已出现每
GB 3 ⾄ 5 美分、即每 TB 30 ⾄ 50 美元的成交价格，部分案例甚⾄更⾼。摩根⼠丹利表⽰，尽管尚
不能将这些现货价格外推⾄全市场，但实际成交数据表明，其⽜市盈利预测所依赖的定价假设
正变得越来越可信。
估值具吸引⼒，⼤摩重申增持
基于上述判断，摩根⼠丹利认为周五的⼤幅回调制造了显著的买⼊机会，⽽⾮论题转折点。
以当前股价计，希捷对应该⾏ CY28 基本情景每股盈利的市盈率为 10 倍，对应⽜市情景仅约 6.8
倍；西部数据对应 CY28 基本情景市盈率为 8.5 倍，对应⽜市情景约 5.8 倍。摩根⼠丹利指出，其
基本情景每股盈利预测仍较市场⼀致预期⾼出约 20% ⾄ 25% 。

⾔，华为未来不得不涨价。
10 ⽉ 5 ⽇，华为发布 9 ⽉ 29 ⽇国际媒体圆桌会议摘要。华为常务董事、产品投资评审委员会主
任、终端 BG 董事⻓余承东会上表⽰，⾃ 2019 年以来，华为持续推进芯⽚、操作系统和基础软件
⾃主研发，并与国内供应链合作制造先进芯⽚。 2023 年，华为重新开始在国内⽣产智能⼿机所
使⽤的芯⽚。余承东表⽰，如今，依靠芯⽚、 HarmonyOS 以及中国本⼟供应链、材料和⼯艺节
点，华为已经基本摆脱对美国技术的依赖。
在芯⽚⽅⾯，华为最新发布的 Mate 90 系列搭载麒麟 9050 Pro ，整机性能较上⼀代提升 31% ；

HarmonyOS 6 和 HarmonyOS 7 ⽬前已运⾏在超过 9000 万台设备上，预计年底突破 1 亿台。与此
同时，华为正在继续扩⼤ LogicFolding 芯⽚的应⽤，并逐步推进 HarmonyOS 全球化。
不过，技术⾃主能⼒提升并未消除成本压⼒。余承东表⽰，内存价格⼤幅上涨已使每部⼿机新
增成本超过 200 美元，华为此前通过⾃⾏吸收部分成本、放缓涨价来减轻消费者压⼒，但整体利
润率已经 “ 急剧下降 ” 。他直⾔，未来华为也 “ 不得不提价 ” 。
从芯⽚到鸿蒙，华为持续扩张⾃主技术与⽣态版图
最新发布的 Mate 90 系列搭载麒麟 9050 Pro 。余承东表⽰，该芯⽚的基带、 ISP 、移动安全处理
器、 CPU 、 GPU 以及采⽤达芬奇架构的 NPU 均由华为⾃主设计。与此同时，华为通过系统级优
化进⼀步提升芯⽚能效和整机性能。
华为也在将 LogicFolding 技术应⽤于更多⼿机。该技术基于 “ 韬定律 ” ，通过垂直堆叠逻辑层、加
强层间⾼密度互连重构电路布局，从⽽缩短关键路径、降低延迟，⽬前已应⽤于麒麟 9050

Pro 。余承东表⽰，未来将有更多华为⼿机采⽤搭载 LogicFolding 技术的芯⽚。
在软件⽣态⽅⾯， HarmonyOS 已成为华为⾃主技术体系的重要组成部分。余承东表⽰，⽬前运
⾏ HarmonyOS 6 和 HarmonyOS 7 的设备已超过 9000 万台，预计今年年底突破 1 亿台。鸿蒙⽣态
的应⽤和服务数量已超过 45 万个，全球注册开发者达到 1100 万⼈，应⽤创新合作伙伴超过 2.3 万
家。
余承东坦⾔，构建⼀个全新的操作系统⽣态 “ 极其困难 ” 。经过多年投⼊，华为已逐步建⽴起⾃
⼰的应⽤和开发者⽣态。
对于海外市场，华为⽬前仍采取循序渐进的策略。余承东表⽰，公司正在考虑逐步将
HarmonyOS 推向全球，但⽬前仍处于初步计划阶段，未来将 “ 慢慢推进、⼀步⼀步 ” 。
内存涨价令单机成本增加超 200 美元，华为利润率⼤幅下滑

真正让华为⾯临压⼒的，是内存成本⼤涨。余承东表⽰，内存价格⼤幅上涨后，每部⼿机新增
成本超过 200 美元。由于华为⼿机采⽤的内存规格较⾼，受到的成本冲击也更明显。

⼰。

Document 624: 日銀金融政策決定会合（2026年9月） ～3カ月で追加利上げ、焦点は今後の利上げペースへ～

Source PDF: all report pdf/日銀金融政策決定会合（2026年9月） ～3カ月で追加利上げ、焦点は今後の利上げペースへ～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-09-18
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

BOJ Watching 
onomic Trends

2026 年 9 月 18 日

1/5 
日銀金融政策決定会合（2026 年 9 月） 
～3 カ月で追加利上げ、焦点は今後の利上げペースへ～ 
 経済調査部 シニアエグゼクティブエコノミスト  新家 義貴 
（℡：050-5474-7490）

（要旨） 
○ 日銀は政策金利を 1.00％から 1.25％へ引き上げた。6 月会合以来 3 カ月ぶりの利上げであ
り、植田総裁就任後では最も短い間隔での追加利上げとなった。 
○ 景気・物価の中心的な見通しやリスク要因に大きな変化はない一方、国内企業物価の高い伸
びや価格転嫁の広がりなどから、これまで警戒してきた物価上振れリスクはより現実味を増
している。 日銀はこれを踏まえ、 従来よりも早めに金融緩和の度合いを調整したとみられる。 
○ 1.25％への利上げ後も金融環境はなお緩和的とされている。政策金利は名目中立金利レンジ
の下限付近に位置しており、追加利上げ余地はなお残る。 
○ 植田総裁会見では、今回の3 カ月間隔の利上げを一時的な前倒しと位置づけるのか、それと
も今後も毎会合を「ライブ」とし、10 月を含めて次回以降の会合で追加利上げを排除しない
姿勢を維持するのかが最大の焦点となる。加えて、1.25％でも金融環境をどの程度緩和的と
みるか、利上げの累積効果を見極めるために時間を置く必要があると考えているかにも注目
したい。 
○ 植田総裁会見前の現時点では、次回は12 月に 1.50％、その後は2027 年3 月に1.75％、6 月
に 2.00％への利上げを予想する。 原油高とその二次波及による物価上振れリスクが当面解消
されにくいことや、対外環境も日銀の正常化を後押しする方向にあることが背景である。

（植田総裁会見前に執筆）

1. 中心シナリオは不変、物価上振れリスクへの警戒を強化 
日本銀行は9 月17、18 日の金融政策決定会合で、政策金利を1.00％から1.25％へ引き上げる
ことを決定した。6 月会合以来3 カ月ぶりの利上げとなり、植田総裁就任後では最も短い間隔での
追加利上げとなった。今回の利上げ自体は事前に市場で完全に織り込まれており、決定そのもの
にサプライズはない。もっとも、これまで半年程度の間隔を空けながら慎重に利上げを進めてき
た日銀が、今回はその間隔を3 カ月に短縮したという点で、政策運営上の意味は大きい。

### Page 2

2/5 
今回の決定は賛成7、反対2 となり、浅田委員と佐藤委員は今回の利上げに反対した。浅田委員
は、わが国の景気が必ずしも強い状況にあるとはいえないことなどから、今回のタイミングでの
利上げは時期尚早と判断した。佐藤委員も、経済・物価情勢がこれまでと比べて大きく加速して
いる状況ではないとして、現時点での利上げに慎重な立場を示した。 
今回の声明文をみると、景気・物価の中心的な見通しやリスク要因そのものに、前回から大き
な変化はない。景気については、中東情勢の影響を受けつつも「緩やかに回復している」との判
断を維持し、先行きについても、原油高などの影響で一時的に成長率が低下するものの、その後
はAI関連需要や政府施策などを背景に緩やかな成長を続けるとの見方を示している。日銀自身
も、経済・物価情勢は「中心的な見通しに概ね沿って推移している」と評価している。 
物価面でも、基調的な物価上昇率が2％の「物価安定の目標」を超えて上振れていくリスクや、
それがその後の経済に悪影響を及ぼさないよう、基調物価を2％程度で安定させていく必要性は、
前回からすでに示されていた。したがって、今回新たな上振れリスクが加わったというよりは、
これまで警戒してきたリスクが、足元のデータによってより現実味を増してきたとみるのが自然
である。 
実際、今回の声明文では、国内企業物価が前年比で高い伸びを続けていることに加え、企業間
取引における価格上昇圧力が消費者段階にも波及し始めていること、中長期の予想物価上昇率が
引き続き上昇していることが指摘された。基調的な物価上昇率が2％に近づくなかで、原油高や既
往のコスト上昇、価格転嫁の広がりなどが重なれば、物価上振れが一時的なものにとどまらず、
より持続的なものになるリスクが高まる。 
こうした点を踏まえると、今回の3 カ月という短い間隔での利上げは、景気・物価の中心シナ
リオが大きく変わったことへの対応というより、これまで想定してきた上振れリスクへの警戒度
合いが高まるなか、日銀がより早い段階で金融緩和の度合いを調整する必要があると判断した結
果とみられる。 
今回の声明文でも、今後について「経済・物価・金融情勢に応じて、引き続き政策金利を引き
上げ、金融緩和の度合いを調整していく」との方針は維持された。また、1.25％への引き上げ後
も「緩和的な金融環境は維持される」と明記されている。政策金利は名目中立金利の推計レンジ
の下限付近に入ったにすぎず、1.25％への利上げ後も金融環境になお緩和余地が残っているとの
認識にあるとみられる。このため、今回の利上げは金融引き締めへの転換ではなく、緩和状態を
維持しながら、物価上振れリスクに対応して金融緩和の度合いを従来よりも早めに縮小したもの
と評価できる。

2. 植田総裁会見の注目点 ～タカ派姿勢をどこまで示せるか～ 
15 時30 分からの植田総裁会見では以下のポイントに注目している。

今後の利上げペース 
今回は6 月から3 カ月での追加利上げとなったが、今回だけ利上げ間隔を3 カ月に短縮したの

### Page 3

3/5 
か、それとも今後も従来よりも短い間隔で利上げを進めるのかが焦点となる。日銀が今後も毎会
合を「ライブ」とし、10 月を含めて次回以降の会合で追加利上げを排除しない姿勢を維持するの
かを確認したい。とりわけ、10 月会合での追加利上げを明確に排除するのかどうかは、今後の利
上げペースを見極めるうえで重要な手掛かりとなる。

金融環境をどの程度「緩和的」と評価するか 
今回の声明文では、1.25％への利上げ後も「緩和的な金融環境は維持される」と明記された。
政策金利は、日銀の自然利子率推計から機械的に算出される名目中立金利のレンジ（1.1～
2.5％）に入ったものの、その下限付近にとどまる。実質金利もなお低く、企業の資金需要や金融
機関の貸出姿勢にも明確な引き締まりはみられない。 
植田総裁が、今回の利上げ後も現在の金融環境になお相応の緩和度合いが残っているとの認識
を示せば、追加利上げ余地は大きく、政策金利の最終的な到達点が1.5％や1.75％を上回る可能
性も意識されよう。一方、中立金利レンジに入ったことや既往の利上げの累積効果を重視する発
言が増えれば、最終的な金利水準はそれほど高くならないとの見方につながる可能性がある。

原油高と円高を踏まえた上で、物価上振れリスクをどう評価するか 
足元では原油価格の上昇が一段と強まる一方、円相場は円高方向に動いており、輸入物価には
相反する力が働いている。会見では、円高による押し下げを踏まえてもなお、原油高によるコス
ト上昇やその二次波及を強く警戒しているのか、また従来よりも物価上振れリスクは高まってい
るのかといった点を確認したい。また、原油高を単なるエネルギー価格上昇や景気下押し要因と
みるのではなく、企業による価格転嫁を通じて基調的な物価にも影響し得るとみているかも重要
である。

原油高の景気への悪影響をどの程度重くみているか 
原油高は物価を押し上げる一方、企業収益や家計の実質所得を圧迫する。ただ、雇用・所得環
境や企業収益がなお底堅いなかで、日銀が景気への下押しを現時点では吸収可能とみているので
あれば、政策判断では物価上振れへの対応をより重視しやすい。景気下押しへの警戒をどの程度
示すかは、今後の利上げペースを考えるうえでの手掛かりとなる。

利上げの累積効果を確認するため、今後は時間を置く必要があるとみているか 
政策金利が中立金利の推計レンジに入ったことで、今後は既往の利上げが企業の借入や設備投
資、住宅ローン、個人消費などに及ぼす影響をこれまで以上に見極める必要がある。植田総裁が
累積的な影響を確認するために一定の時間が必要との姿勢を示せば、次回利上げまでの間隔を広
げる方向のメッセージとなる。一方、これまでの利上げによる経済への影響は限定的で、金融環
境はなお十分に緩和的との評価を示せば、今後も比較的短い間隔で追加利上げを続ける余地があ
ることを示唆することになる。

### Page 4

4/5

為替と海外金融政策をどう位置づけるか 
先日の米ＦＯＭＣがタカ派的と受け止められたことで、日銀が慎重なメッセージを出せば円安
圧力が再び強まる可能性がある。一方、日銀は為替そのものを政策目標としているわけではない
ため、為替水準そのものを理由に金融政策を説明することは避けるとみられる。円相場を輸入物
価や物価上振れリスクを通じた判断材料としてどう説明するか、また米国を含む海外中銀の引き
締めが日本の金融環境に与える影響をどうみているかを確認したい。

2027 年春闘の位置づけ 
現時点では物価上振れリスクが政策判断の中心にあるが、賃金上昇の持続性は基調的な物価動
向を判断するうえで引き続き重要である。秋以降、2027 年春闘の初動に関する情報が段階的に増
えてくる。日銀がこうした情報を今後の利上げ判断にどの程度織り込む考えなのかも確認した
い。

3. 12 月利上げを予想（植田総裁会見前） 
植田総裁の記者会見前の現時点では、次回は12 月会合で政策金利を1.50％へ引き上げると予想
している。今回、日銀は前回利上げから3 カ月という短い間隔で追加利上げに踏み切った。基調
的な物価上昇率が2％に近づくなか、これまで警戒してきた物価上振れリスクがより現実味を増し
ており、日銀は上振れリスクが明確に顕在化するのを待つのではなく、従来よりも早めに政策対
応を行う姿勢を強めているとみられる。 
先行きも、物価上振れリスクは容易には解消されないだろう。円高は輸入物価の押し下げ要因
となる一方、円高による物価押し下げよりも、足元の原油高とその二次波及による上振れリスク
の方が大きいとみられる。原油高はエネルギー価格を直接押し上げるだけでなく、物流費、包装
資材、化学製品、食料品などを通じて川下価格へ波及する可能性がある。筆者は年末～年初にか
けてＣＰＩコアは＋３％を上回ると予想しており、物価上振れへの警戒感は一段と強まるだろ
う。 
また、1.25％への利上げ後も金融環境はなお緩和的とみられる。政策金利は名目中立金利レン
ジの下限付近に入るが、実質金利はなおマイナスであり、現時点で企業の資金需要や貸出環境に
明確な引き締まりはみられない。このため、1.25％到達をもって利上げ余地が大きく狭まるとは
考えにくい。 
もっとも、10 月会合での連続利上げは現時点ではメインシナリオとしていない。9 月利上げか
ら 1 カ月しかなく、新たに確認できる情報が限られるためである。その点、12 月会合までには、
秋以降の物価動向、原油価格、為替相場、企業の価格設定行動、賃金動向などをより幅広く確認
できる。2027 年春闘に向けた初期情報も徐々に増えてくることから、追加利上げを判断するタイ
ミングとしては12 月が最も自然だとみている。 
その後については、2027 年 3 月に1.75％、6 月に2.00％へ引き上げるとの予想を維持する。原

### Page 5

経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。
5/5
油高の影響はエネルギー価格にとどまらず、物流費や包装資材、食料品、日用品などへ時間差を
伴って波及するとみられるほか、企業の価格転嫁姿勢も積極化している。資源高がラグをもって
電気・ガス代の上昇に繋がることもあり、物価上振れリスクは年明け以降も当面解消されにくい
とみている。
対外環境も日銀の追加利上げを後押しする方向にある。米国で追加利上げ観測が強まるなか、
日銀の利上げ期待が後退すれば、円安圧力が再び強まりかねない。加えて、米国側も円安抑制に
向けた日本の金融政策正常化を支持する姿勢を示しており、日銀が国内の経済・物価情勢に応じ
て利上げを進めるうえで、対外面からの制約は以前より小さいと考えられる。
こうしたことから、日銀は基調的な物価上昇率を2％程度で安定させるため、今後も段階的な利

上げを継続すると予想する。

涉事阿曼籍副驾驶系 “ 独狼 ” 作案，其⼊职前就已蓄谋，专⻔应聘⻜往特拉维夫航线。事发

摘要：
新华社消息，据以⾊列媒体 4 ⽇报道，以⾊列⽅⾯的初步调查显⽰，在迪拜航空公司客机驾驶舱

机撞向以⾊列特拉维夫的摩天⼤楼。
迪拜航空机⻓与内塔尼亚胡通话，回忆遭袭细节
央视新闻消息， 9 ⽉ 30 ⽇，阿联酋迪拜航空公司⼀架⻜往以⾊列的航班发⽣副驾驶袭击机⻓事
件。主导调查的阿联酋检⽅ 10 ⽉ 3 ⽇称，涉事副驾驶企图实施 “ 恐怖活动 ” 。
10 ⽉ 3 ⽇，发⽣安全事件的迪拜航空客机机⻓与以⾊列总理内塔尼亚胡通电话。机⻓斯⽶特 · ⻢

奇切尔讲述了⾃⼰遭袭时的细节。
迪拜航空遭袭机⻓斯⽶特 · ⻢奇切尔：（事发时）我正⽬视前⽅，没有回头看。突然，我想⼤概是
过了四五分钟，我的后脑勺遭到重击，我当时的第⼀反应是⻜机出了严重故障，情况不妙。紧
接着，⼜打过来了，⼀下接⼀下，我这才意识到，不是⻜机出了问题，⽽是跟我同在驾驶舱⾥
的那个⼈。我试图阻⽌他，但由于他最初的⼏下重击，我的视线⼏乎完全模糊，什么也看不清

⼰必须进⾏殊死搏⽃，不管这是为了我⾃⼰还是为了其他⼈，总之，我知道如果不这么做，就

⻜机控制权。
内塔尼亚胡称赞⻢奇切尔 “ 在绝境中保持冷静判断的能⼒ ” ，并邀请他访问以⾊列。

ユーロ圏景気概況① 景気は拡大している
ユーロ圏の4-6月期の実質GDP（確定値）は前期比+0.6%と、1-3月期（同横ばい）から反発した。需要項目別には、前期
にマイナス寄与だった純輸出が大幅なプラス寄与に転じて景気をけん引した。一方、最新9月の景況感指数は97.9と低下
に転じた。酷暑によるさまざまな調整圧力や政情不安（特にフランス）が景気の重荷になっているもよう。

ユーロ圏景気概況② 生産は低迷している
ユーロ圏の6月の鉱工業生産は前月比▲0.1%と2カ月連続で減少し、均した動きでも下向きに。非耐久財が減産に転じ
て生産全体の重荷となった。一方、同月の名目輸出は同+1.2%と増勢が前月（同+0.7%）から加速し、均した動きでも上
向き。ゆえに生産の低迷は内需向けが主因であるもよう。

英国景気概況⑦ 金利は上昇、株価は下落
英国の9月の長期金利は上昇した。インフレ加速に対する懸念から長期金利は上昇した。一方、同月の株価は下落した。
イラン情勢の緊迫化が続き、インフレ加速に対する懸念が高まる中で、株は売りが先行した。
長短金利（10年債と3カ月債） 株価（FT30指数）
（出所）Financial Times（出所）BOE

### Page 17

16 Mitsubishi UFJ Research and Consulting
英国景気概況⑧ ポンドは対ドル・対円ともに下落
英国の通貨ポンドの9月の相場は対ドル・対円ともに下落した。バーナム新首相に対する期待に伴うポンド買いが一巡し、
売りが先行した。フランス大統領選の懸念から進んだユーロ安にも連動した。経常収支は、名目GDPの2%を超える赤字
が定着しており、実需面からのポンド買い圧力は弱い。
ポンド相場 経常収支
（出所）BOE、ONS（出所）BOE

### Page 1

研究报告·宏观策略研究 
海外宏观周报（2026 年第 37 期）

美联储加息预期走升，美债收益率持续走高 
【一周焦点】 
上周美债收益率持续走高， 一度令市场措手不及。从原因看主要有：
一是美国经济意外走强，PMI 数据的大幅走升，核心资本品订单强
劲，反映当前利率水平或仍不足以将美国金融条件拉至紧缩，加息
必要性增加。二是 AI 投资预期并未减弱，长期实际利率仍有支撑。
三是中东局势一度有缓和的希望，但市场并不认可，至周末则再次
转向对抗，红海危机升级，国际油价保持高位。四是美联储官员接
二连三的鹰派发言，年内再加一次息已基本被市场完全定价，且时
点正提前至 10 月。五是美债供给压力持续， 而美财政部的回购规模
再次未满足 60 亿上限。六是全球主要经济体债券同步抛售的溢出
效应，及市场交易顺周期的交易行为。 
【交易模式】 
中东局势紧张，通胀交易持续主导 
【关键数据】 
美国：核心资本品新订单大涨，PMI 全面超预期，新屋销售环比量
价齐升，芝加哥联储全国经济活动指数小幅下行，密歇根大学消费
者信心指数小幅上修。欧洲：消费者信心下滑，PMI 整体回暖。日
本：PMI 景气度下降。 
【重要事件】 
美国：中美达成八点成果共识，美伊谈判前景再度波折。欧洲：瑞
士央行维持零利率不变，欧央行警告能源价格危机。日本：央行前
官员预计未来每个季度都加息。其他地区：中东战火扩大到红海，
OECD 提示通胀风险。 
【本周关注】

### Page 3

【一周焦点】
美联储加息预期走升，美债收益率持续走高
上周美债收益率持续走高， 一度令市场措手不及。10 年期美债收益率最高升至
5.228%，并站稳 5.1%上方，30 年美债收益率最高超过 5.53%。由于短端收益
率和 TIPS 美债收益率均同步上行，期限溢价和长期通胀贡献不明显，收益率
升高主要来源于实际利率抬升和美联储加息预期。CME“美联储观察” 工具显
示，10 月加息预期已由此前的低于 50%升至 60%以上，而 2027 年再加息一
次的概率也明显增加。从原因看主要有以下六方面：
一是美国经济意外走强。上周三美国 PMI 数据的大幅走升成为美债收益率升
高的首要推手。数据显示美国 9 月综合 PMI 攀升至 58.4，预期仅为 55.3，创
2021 年 7 月以来最高水平。服务业与制造业 PMI 分别达到 58.7 和 57.0，内需
推动下的新订单加速，积压订单增速为 2022 年 5 月以来最快，就业增速则创
2022 年 6 月以来新高。PMI 数据表明，美国商业活动在强劲需求推动下加速

扩张。此外，8 月核心资本品季调环比上涨 1.6%，同比则加速至 14.1%，明显
好于市场预期，7 月环比也从 0.4%上修到 0.6%，显示企业设备投资动能在下
半年继续走强。地产方面，新屋销售在高利率下依旧有韧性，8 月新屋销售 68.4
万套， 大幅高于61.5 万套的预期， 季调环比反弹至6.4%， 尽管数据并不算 “强
劲”，但也反映出高利率下的韧性。
美国经济的超预期表现，反映了当前利率水平或仍不足以将美国金融条件拉至
紧缩。正如沃什在 9 月加息时所言， 判断金融条件的宽松与紧缩需要更加注重
观察经济的实际走势，比理论测算中性利率更可靠。而当前的美国经济，表明
美联储的进一步加息仍有必要，也成为市场提高加息预期的坚实理由。
二是 AI 投资预期并未减弱， 长期实际利率仍有支撑。此前业界对于 AI 安全性
问题的争议，一度引发关于 AI 投资或有所放缓的忧虑。但本周特朗普在联大
会议上的讲话再次力挺 AI， 并将AI 改名为 “超级智能” （Super Intelligence）。

在随后的中美元首对话中， 两国同意建立中美人工智能对话，交流人工智能相
关风险和惠益，后续将建立沟通渠道。此表态相对中性，从市场理解看，偏向
于认为两国为避免恶性竞争，在发展 AI 和风险管控之间寻求平衡，而非一味
限制 AI 发展。相信中美对 AI 产业均高度重视，任何一方都难以放缓发展。

### Page 4

三是中东局势一度有缓和的希望，但市场并不认可，至周末则再次转向对抗。
上周中，市场一度爆出伊朗有意重启谈判时间表， 并已向美国提交了相关条件，
争取七天后将重开海峡。国际油价一度回落，但债券市场对此并不认可，表现
出对中东局势反复的厌倦心理。至周末，特朗普被曝已拒绝伊朗提议，并表示
可能在中期选举后恢复对伊朗轰炸。同时，胡塞武装上周继续袭击沙特，土耳
其与巴基斯坦正考虑军事介入，沙特石油生产与运输，以及红海航运继续受到
影响，国际现货原油价格保持在 120 美元/桶上方。
四是美联储官员接二连三的鹰派发言。上周美联储多位官员呼吁进一步收紧政
策， 包括中性和鹰派官员在内的巴尔、哈马克、巴尔金、穆萨莱姆、古尔斯比、
卡什卡利等官员均表态将全力应对通胀压力，强调仍然有加息的必要。即使是
相对鸽派的威廉姆斯、保尔森也表示可能需要进一步加息。导致目前年内再加
一次息已基本被市场完全定价，且加息时点正在由 12 月提前至 10 月。
五是美债供给压力持续，而美财政部的回购规模再次未满足 60 亿上限。上周

美国财政部最新一期长期国债流动性回购规模达到 40.78 亿美元，保持在原先
20 亿的 2 倍的规模而非承诺上限的 3 倍， 而市场的投标量达到104.68 亿美元。
此外，同日美国财政部发行 440 亿美元 7 年期国债，中标收益率达到 5.085%，
创 2009 年该期限债券重新发行以来新高。市场对财政赤字持续扩张的担忧进
一步放大了供给端压力，直接推动长债收益率冲高。
六是全球主要经济体债券同步抛售的溢出效应，以及市场交易顺周期的交易行
为。本轮美债收益率走高并非孤立行情，日本 10 年期日债收益率同步升至
3.073%（1996 年以来新高） ， 英国、 德国长债收益率也同步上行， 全球无风险
利率中枢集体抬升，进一步放大了美债的抛售压力。此外，长债价格下跌会触
发持有长久期资产机构（养老金、保险公司）的久期对冲，被动抛售长债，形
成负反馈循环，从而加大美债抛售压力。
展望后市，预计 10 年期美债基准情景大概率在 5.1%附近高位震荡， 短期存在
冲高至 5.2%–5.3% 的风险， 但持续单边上行空间有限。长端收益率的核心定

价走向依旧关联于美联储的政策预期，本周即将公布的就业与通胀数据极为关
键。而财政供给压力、 海外长线买盘减弱、AI 长久期融资需求、 中东地缘带来
的通胀尾部风险也将对美债收益率形成综合性影响。

### Page 10

【重要事件】
美国：中美达成八点成果共识，美伊谈判前景再度波折
中美两国元首半年内实现国事互访。当地时间 9 月 23 日至 25 日， 中国国家主
席习近平对美国进行国事访问，美国总统特朗普来到安德鲁斯空军基地，迎接
到访。两国元首就中美建设性战略稳定关系及中东局势、乌克兰危机、朝鲜半
岛等重大国际地区问题深入交换意见，中美两军还同意尽快签署加强危机沟通
与预防的谅解备忘录，下次对话将于今年 11 月举行。
中美达成八点成果共识。1. 双方同意构建 “基于尊重、 公平、 对等的中美建设
性战略稳定关系”。2. 双方同意相互支持对方办好亚太经合组织（APEC）领
导人非正式会议和二十国集团（G20）领导人峰会。两国元首有意出席对方主
办的会议。3. 两国元首同意伊朗应履行不发展核武器的承诺， 任何国家或机构
都不得对国际水道征收通行费。4. 两国元首忆及中国和美国是二战盟友， 并肩
作战赢得战争胜利。5. 两国元首认可中美经贸磋商机制积极作用和双方经贸团
队磋商成果，包括建立并推进贸易理事会等机制，达成“300 亿美元”对等降

税安排， 延期吉隆坡经贸磋商成果等， 并指示予以落实。6. 中美禁毒执法部门
合作取得可视化成果。近期，双方密切合作，联合破获多起涉新精神活性物质
及前体化学品案件， 在两国抓获数十名相关犯罪嫌疑人。7. 双方同意建立中美
人工智能对话，交流人工智能相关风险和惠益。下次对话将于今年11 月举行。
双方同意建立人工智能事件的沟通渠道。8. 美方欢迎中方租借给美国亚特兰大
动物园的一对大熊猫抵美。
中美两国经贸团队在美国举行第八轮经贸磋商。双方围绕落实已有经贸磋商共
识、对等降税安排、建立贸易理事会和投资理事会、吉隆坡经贸磋商联合安排
延期等双方共同关注的经贸问题，进行了坦诚、深入、富有建设性的交流，达
成多项共识。 何立峰副总理与贝森特财长作为中美各自牵头人在中美经贸磋商
机制下就人工智能进行了首次对话。下一步，中方将与美方在中美经贸磋商机
制下，就后续工作保持密切沟通。
美伊谈判前景再次出现波折。特朗普 9 月 26 日称，已拒绝伊朗重开霍尔木兹
海峡的提议。他在接受媒体采访时表示，美方完全控制了霍尔木兹海峡，大量

石油正从霍尔木兹海峡流出。伊朗希望达成协议，立即重开霍尔木兹海峡，但
“我拒绝了他们的提议”。美国官员表示，预计将在 11 月中期选举后恢复对

### Page 11

伊朗轰炸。此前多方曾证实，伊朗已向美国递交新的谈判草案，若美方接受条
件，时间表可启动，七天后将重开海峡，同时双方恢复更广泛的和谈。条件其
中包括伊朗重新开放霍尔木兹海峡、美国解除对伊朗经济封锁，伊朗还可能获
得部分被冻结资产。伊朗总统佩泽希齐扬曾在 9 月 25 日表示，支持恢复伊美
谅解备忘录，将为实质性谈判创造条件。但在美国表示拒绝伊朗重开海峡的提
议后，他在接受采访时表示，鉴于每一轮谈判后美国都会发动袭击并实施制裁，
伊朗不再信任与美国的对话。
美伊总统分别在联合国大会一般性辩论上发表讲话。特朗普在开幕当天讲话中
称可迅速“摧毁”伊朗，但不排除双方在 11 月美国国会中期选举后达成结束
冲突协议的可能性。他为美国与以色列 2 月底联合对伊朗发动袭击进行辩护，
称绝不会允许伊朗获得核武器。伊朗总统佩泽希齐扬发表讲话，他表示，伊朗
愿意谈判与合作，但不会接受以强权、制裁或军事威胁迫使其让步。在伊朗受
到制裁的情况下，不能允许霍尔木兹海峡被自由使用。
伊朗议会国家安全委员会已批准有关霍尔木兹海峡的新法律条款，根据这些条

款，违反海峡通行规定的船只，除需缴纳相当于货物价值20%的罚款外，还将
被暂时扣押，直至罚款缴清；伊朗将设立专门的司法部门分支确保规定执行，
伊朗武装部队将每三个月报告执行情况。
美国财政部 20 至 30 年期美债回购未达 60 亿美元上限。美国财政部表示，在
9 月 24 日进行了价值 40.78 亿美元的 20 至 30 年期美国国债流动性回购操作，
回购规模处于 9 月 23 日披露区间的低端。20 年至 30 年期美债回购操作收到
104.68 亿美元投标。同日，美国财政部发行 440 亿美元 7 年期国债，中标收益
率为 5.085%，创 2009 年该期限债券重新发行以来新高。
特朗普联合国讲话力挺 AI 发展。特朗普表示，明确反对通过国际协议限制技
术发展，称美国完全拒绝任何旨在建立全球性机制来管控 AI 的企图。特朗普
对“前沿模型”可能失控所带来的风险不以为意，称美国将继续推动AI 发展，
不想扼杀一种可能“比工业革命还要伟大”的技术。今后美国政府文件将考虑
用“超级智能”这个词替代“人工智能”这一称呼。

美联储多位官员呼吁进一步收紧政策。克利夫兰联储主席哈玛克表示，她担心
持续高企的通胀可能会让美国公众逐渐将高物价视作常态， 而美联储绝不能让
这种情况发生。美联储理事巴尔表示，可能需要进一步加息以确保通胀回归目

### Page 12

标水平。里奇蒙德联储主席巴尔金表示，美联储本月加息将有助于降低通胀，
但没有暗示是否需要进一步收紧政策。通胀冲击可能需要一段时间才能消退，
持续高企的价格压力存在变得根深蒂固的风险。除数据中心和人工智能领域
外，其他领域也呈现出增长势头，消费者支出保持稳定，国防和制造业表现强
劲。圣路易斯联储主席穆萨莱姆表示，如果没有进一步的政策限制， 通胀在18
个月后仍有可能大幅高于 2%的目标。利率可能需要进一步上升，以抑制由需
求和供应驱动的通胀；加息最好是“提前且逐步的”，而不是“延后且大幅度
的”。芝加哥联储主席古尔斯比表示，近年来供给冲击出现得更频繁、持续时
间也更长，美联储不能再将其简单视为暂时性因素“看穿”。如果供给端的价
格压力持续向更广泛领域蔓延，央行仍需通过收紧政策压制通胀。明尼阿波利
斯联储主席卡什卡利称，美国通胀仍然过高， 压力已经超出伊朗战争导致的油
价冲击， 扩散至经济多个领域美联储越来越担心通胀并非只集中在受中东冲突
或关税影响的领域，服务业也出现通胀迹象。纽约联储主席威廉姆斯表示，今

年再度加息是一个 “合理” 预期， 美联储依靠明确前瞻指引的时代已经 “结束” 。
费城联储主席保尔森称，若经济走势符合预期， 美联储可能需要进一步加息以
推动通胀回归目标水平。
美联储正在制定一项计划，拟提高触发对大型银行实施更严格监管的资产门
槛，此举将使部分贷款机构免于承担昂贵的额外监管成本，并可能推动行业整
合。知情人士称，美联储预计将很快提议重新调整银行需接受资产负债表压力
测试、流动性、资本及其他更严格监管规则的门槛，以反映通胀和经济增长因
素。
美国政府正酝酿针对油价高企的措施。据媒体报道，特朗普政府官员正探讨一
系列措施，包括暂停征收燃油税，以应对柴油价格高涨。美国副总统万斯、能
源部长赖特、内政部长伯格姆和财长贝森特等高层都参与了有关讨论。
欧洲：瑞士央行维持零利率不变，欧央行警告能源价格危机
瑞士央行将政策利率维持在 0%不变，符合市场预期。 瑞士 8 月通胀率为
0.8%，仍落在央行 0%‑2% 的通胀目标区间之内。不过，市场观察人士认为，
瑞士央行被迫加息只是时间问题。瑞士央行预期，通胀率可能还会在高位维持

一段时间。

### Page 13

欧央行警告能源价格危机。欧洲中央银行副行长武伊契奇 25 日也警告，由于
全球炼油产能缩减，柴油价格可能持续居高，推高欧元区的通货膨胀。欧盟能
源专员约根森周五致函成员国的能源部长，警告欧盟正面临能源价格危机，呼
吁他们采取措施抑制天然气需求。目前，欧盟尚未面对供应短缺的问题，但价
格持续高涨，迫使一些成员国预先储存天然气，以备冬季需求。欧洲央行管委
科赫尔表示，欧洲经济表现出的韧性超出预期， 这表明如果进一步收紧政策仍
有必要，欧洲经济能够承受更高利率。
欧盟成员国就延长对俄制裁达成一致， 同意将相关制裁措施延长三年。根据协
议，欧盟将把对 3000 多个俄罗斯个人和实体实施制裁的期限延长 36 个月，同
时将两名俄罗斯企业家移出制裁名单。
俄高官称美俄乌三方谈判或在近期举行。俄罗斯总统新闻秘书佩斯科夫 9 月 25
日表示，俄罗斯、美国、乌克兰三方会晤可能在近期举行，但目前没有具体细
节。佩斯科夫还确认了俄总统特别代表德米特里耶夫访美的事宜。
日本：央行前官员预计未来每个季度都加息
前日本央行审议委员预计明年 6 月前每个季度都加息。前日本央行审议委员樱

井真 9 月 24 日表示，为应对日益加剧的通胀压力，预计日本央行将大约每三
个月加息一次，并在明年 6 月左右将利率上调至 2%。与 2 月份美国针对伊朗
采取行动前的水平相比，日本近几个月的原油进口成本飙升了约70%至 80%，
这将推高未来的消费端通胀。随着 9 月份将利率上调至 1.25%（创 31 年来新
高），日本央行的政策重心已发生转变，日益侧重于应对由燃料成本飙升引发
的范围不断扩大的价格上涨压力。
其他地区：中东战火扩大到红海，OECD 提示通胀风险
中东战火正在从波斯湾和霍尔木兹海峡扩大到红海。伊朗最高领袖顾问萨法维
表示，下一阶段的潜在行动范围可能扩大至印度洋及其他地区。若美国发动新
的军事行动，红海和曼德海峡或将成为新的作战区域。也门武装部队宣称，已
对沙特首都利雅得和沙特阿美位于延布的设施发动两次军事行动， 使用弹道导
弹、巡航导弹和无人机，行动取得成功。沙特阿拉伯、土耳其和巴基斯坦三国
军方领导人 25 日在沙特首都利雅得举行会议，表示将立即着手把《麦加共同
防务协议》 相关承诺转化为切实有效的军事合作。巴土两国均未宣布直接参加

### Page 14

对胡塞作战。9 月 24 日， 沙特称拦截了6 枚飞向塔伊夫和延布的弹道导弹。 胡
塞方面则宣称袭击了利雅得一处“敏感目标”和延布的沙特阿美设施。
经合组织（OECD）发布最新经济展望报告，小幅上调 2026 年全球增长预期，
但也提示通胀压力增大，以及地缘政治、气候和债务风险。OECD 预计，美联
储今年还将加息一次；欧元区、澳大利亚和韩国将进一步小幅加息，日本也将
进一步上调政策利率。报告认为，AI 相关投资、 生产、 贸易成为支持增长的重
要力量，但受到中东冲突影响，能源价格再度抬升将增大通胀压力，对全球经
济产生扰动。
国际货币基金组织（IMF）发布最新年度报告指出，今年由私人部门主导的全
球人工智能 （AI） 投资规模可能突破2 万亿美元，成为近年来推动经济增长的
最强劲动力之一。但随着相关基建及项目规模持续扩大，部分高成本投资越来
越依赖债务融资。一旦未来回报未能达到预期，恐会引发资产估值显著调整、
财富缩水及企业裁员等连锁效应。
加拿大央行行长麦克勒姆表示，如果通胀压力显示出更加持久的迹象， 决策者

不希望在加息问题上行动过慢。随着中东冲突推高能源价格，加拿大通胀维持
高位的风险正在上升。
